The bulk-boundary correspondence for the Einstein equations in asymptotically Anti-de Sitter spacetimes
Gustav Holzegel, Arick Shao

Abstract
. In this paper, we consider vacuum asymptotically anti-de Sitter spacetimes ( ℳ , g ) (\mathscr{M},g) with conformal boundary ( ℐ , 𝔤 ) (\mathscr{I},\mathfrak{g}) . We establish a correspondence, near ℐ \mathscr{I} , between such spacetimes and their conformal boundary data on ℐ \mathscr{I} . More specifically, given a domain 𝒟 ⊂ ℐ \mathscr{D}\subset\mathscr{I} , we prove that the coefficients 𝔤 ( 0 ) = 𝔤 \mathfrak{g}^{\scriptscriptstyle(0)}=\mathfrak{g} and 𝔤 ( n ) \mathfrak{g}^{\scriptscriptstyle(n)} (the undetermined term , or stress energy tensor ) in a Fefferman-Graham expansion of the metric g g from the boundary uniquely determine g g near 𝒟 \mathscr{D} , provided 𝒟 \mathscr{D} satisfies a generalised null convexity condition (GNCC). The GNCC is a conformally invariant criterion on 𝒟 \mathscr{D} , first identified by Chatzikaleas and the second author, that ensures a foliation of pseudoconvex hypersurfaces in ℳ \mathscr{M} near 𝒟 \mathscr{D} , and with the pseudoconvexity degenerating in the limit at 𝒟 \mathscr{D} . As a corollary of this result, we deduce that conformal symmetries of ( 𝔤 ( 0 ) , 𝔤 ( n ) ) (\mathfrak{g}^{\scriptscriptstyle(0)},\mathfrak{g}^{\scriptscriptstyle(n)}) on domains 𝒟 ⊂ ℐ \mathscr{D}\subset\mathscr{I} satisfying the GNCC extend to spacetime symmetries near 𝒟 \mathscr{D} . The proof, which does not require any analyticity assumptions, relies on three key ingredients: (1) a calculus of vertical tensor-fields developed for this setting; (2) a novel system of transport and wave equations for differences of metric and curvature quantities; and (3) recently established Carleman estimates for tensorial wave equations near the conformal boundary.



1.
Introduction

Asymptotically anti-de Sitter
(abbreviated
aAdS
) solutions to the
(n+1)
-dimensional Einstein-vacuum equations with negative cosmological constant,

(1.1)

\operatorname{Ric}[g]=-ng\text{,}

are spacetimes whose asymptotic geometry models the maximally symmetric solution of (
1.1
),
anti-de Sitter
(AdS) space.
Recall AdS spacetime can be globally represented as

(1.2)

(\mathbb{R}^{4},g_{\textrm{AdS}})\text{,}\qquad g_{\textrm{AdS}}:=-(1+r^{2})dt^{2}+(1+r^{2})^{-1}dr^{2}+r^{2}\mathring{\gamma}_{n-1}\text{,}

where
g_{\textrm{AdS}}
is expressed in polar coordinates, and where
\mathring{\gamma}_{n-1}
is the unit round metric on
\mathbb{S}^{n-1}
.

The distinguishing feature of aAdS spacetimes, in contrast to asymptotically flat settings, is the existence of a timelike conformal boundary at infinity.
This undermines global hyperbolicity, requiring the specification of suitable boundary conditions in addition to Cauchy data for a well-posed dynamical formulation of (
1.1
); see
[
21
,
23
]
.
Globally, this leads to very rich behaviour and requires understanding an entire range of novel phenomena, such as superradiant instabilities
[
16
]
and stable trapping
[
32
]
in the case of aAdS black holes.
In particular, the nonlinear (in)stability properties of AdS spacetime and the Kerr-AdS family of black holes are still not known, although considerable progress has been made on various model problems, most notably the recent breakthrough
[
46
]
.

Asymptotically AdS spacetimes have also seen a resurgence of interest in the physics literature, in view of the AdS/CFT conjecture
[
28
,
42
,
54
]
, which, roughly, posits a correspondence between the gravitational dynamics in the aAdS spacetime interior and a conformal field theory on the boundary.
Despite its prominence in physics, there are relatively few rigorous mathematical statements pertaining to the AdS/CFT correspondence, especially in dynamical settings.
In fact, almost all known rigorous results have been in stationary or static contexts; see, e.g.,
[
2
,
12
,
13
,
14
,
55
]
.

In this paper, we formulate and prove a purely classical version of this correspondence, relating the geometry of the conformal boundary to the interior geometry near the boundary.
In particular, this serves as the first such rigorous result for dynamical (time-dependent) aAdS spacetimes.

1.1.
Fefferman-Graham Expansions

As our main interests lie near the conformal boundary, it will be useful to express aAdS metrics in a form that centres the boundary geometry.
In the case of AdS spacetime, one convenient method for achieving this is to apply the change of coordinate

4r:=\rho^{-1}(2+\rho)(2-\rho)\text{,}\qquad\rho\in(0,2]\text{,}

which transforms (
1.2
) into the so-called
Fefferman-Graham gauge
:
1
1

1

A different possibility is the more well-known conformal embedding of AdS spacetime into half the Einstein cylinder. However, the Fefferman-Graham gauge is a more convenient form for studying general aAdS metrics.

(1.3)

g_{\textrm{AdS}}=\rho^{-2}\left[d\rho^{2}+(-dt^{2}+\mathring{\gamma}_{n-1})-\frac{1}{2}\rho^{2}(dt^{2}+\mathring{\gamma}_{n-1})+\frac{1}{16}\rho^{4}(-dt^{2}+\mathring{\gamma}_{n-1})\right]\text{.}

For general aAdS geometries, one can apply a similar transformation into a Fefferman-Graham (
FG
) gauge, characterized by a boundary defining function
\rho
that is both normalised and fully decoupled from the other components.
2
2

2

See
[
25
]
for a treatment of asymptotically hyperbolic manifolds—the Riemannian analogue of our setting. In fact, the transformation to FG gauges in
[
25
]
extends directly to Lorentzian, aAdS settings.

As a result, in this paper, we will
define
the
aAdS
spacetimes that we consider in terms of such FG gauges.
We refer to these as
FG-aAdS segments
, representing an appropriate near-boundary spacetime patch along with adapted coordinates:

Definition 1.1
.

Let
(\mathscr{I},\mathfrak{g})
be a smooth
n
-dimensional Lorentzian manifold, and let
\rho_{0}>0
.
We say that
(\mathscr{M}:=(0,\rho_{0}]\times\mathscr{I},g)
is a
vacuum FG-aAdS segment
, with
conformal infinity

(\mathscr{I},\mathfrak{g})
, if
g
satisfies the Einstein-vacuum equations (
1.1
), and it can be expressed in the FG gauge,

(1.4)

g=\rho^{-2}[d\rho^{2}+\mathsf{g}(\rho)]\text{,}

where
\mathsf{g}(\rho)
,
\rho\in(0,\rho_{0}]
is a smooth family of Lorentzian metrics on
\mathscr{I}
(i.e. a
vertical metric
) that also extends continuously as a Lorentzian metric to
\{0\}\times\mathscr{I}
, and with
\mathsf{g}(0)=\mathfrak{g}
.

The reader is referred to Section
2.1
for a more detailed development of FG-aAdS segments, as well as for precise definitions.
In particular, observe from (
1.3
) that (time strips of) AdS spacetime can itself be expressed as a vacuum FG-aAdS segment, with the standard conformal infinity

(1.5)

(\mathscr{I}_{\textrm{AdS}},\mathfrak{g}_{\textrm{AdS}}):=((T_{-},T_{+})\times\mathbb{S}^{n-1},-dt^{2}+\mathring{\gamma}_{n-1})\text{,}\qquad T_{-}<T_{+}\text{.}

More generally, a large class of vacuum FG-aAdS segments with conformal infinity (
1.5
) arises by solving a boundary-initial value problem for the Einstein-vacuum equations; see
[
21
,
23
]
.

If
(\mathscr{M},g)
is a vacuum FG-aAdS segment, with conformal infinity
(\mathscr{I},\mathfrak{g})
, then the Einstein-vacuum equations imply the following formal series expansion for
g
near
\rho=0
:

(1.6)

\displaystyle\mathsf{g}(\rho)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\mathfrak{g}^{\scriptscriptstyle(0)}+\mathfrak{g}^{\scriptscriptstyle(2)}\rho^{2}+\dots+\mathfrak{g}^{\scriptscriptstyle(n-1)}\rho^{n-1}+\mathfrak{g}^{\scriptscriptstyle(n)}\rho^{n}+\dots&n\text{ odd,}\\
\mathfrak{g}^{\scriptscriptstyle(0)}+\mathfrak{g}^{\scriptscriptstyle(2)}\rho^{2}+\dots+\mathfrak{g}^{\scriptscriptstyle(n-2)}\rho^{n-2}+\mathfrak{g}^{\scriptscriptstyle(\star)}\rho^{n}\log\rho+\mathfrak{g}^{\scriptscriptstyle(n)}\rho^{n}+\dots&n\text{ even,}\\
\end{cases}

where the
\mathfrak{g}^{\scriptscriptstyle(k)}
’s and
\mathfrak{g}^{\scriptscriptstyle(\star)}
are tensor fields on
\mathscr{I}
.
Note that the leading coefficient
\mathfrak{g}^{\scriptscriptstyle(0)}=\mathfrak{g}
is simply the boundary metric.
3
3

3

We will use the notations
\mathfrak{g}
and
\mathfrak{g}^{\scriptscriptstyle(0)}
interchangeably, depending on context.

Furthermore, the Einstein-vacuum equations imply that all coefficients
\mathfrak{g}^{\scriptscriptstyle(k)}
for
0<k<n
, as well as
\mathfrak{g}^{\scriptscriptstyle(\star)}
when
n
is even, are determined locally by
\mathfrak{g}
and its derivatives.
In particular, for
n\geq 3
,
-\mathfrak{g}^{\scriptscriptstyle(2)}
is precisely the Schouten tensor of
\mathfrak{g}
, namely,

\mathcal{P}[\mathfrak{g}]:=\frac{1}{n-2}\left(\operatorname{Ric}[\mathfrak{g}]-\frac{1}{2(n-1)}\operatorname{R}[\mathfrak{g}]\cdot\mathfrak{g}\right)\text{.}

For the coefficient
\mathfrak{g}^{\scriptscriptstyle(n)}
, the Einstein-vacuum equations imply that there exist universal functions
\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G}
—depending only on the boundary dimension
n
---such that
4
4

4

Moreover, both
\mathcal{F}
and
\mathcal{G}
are identically zero if
n
is odd.

(1.7)

\operatorname{div}_{\mathfrak{g}}\mathfrak{g}^{\scriptscriptstyle(n)}=\mathcal{F}(\mathfrak{g},\partial\mathfrak{g},\dots,\partial^{n+1}\mathfrak{g})\text{,}\qquad\operatorname{tr}_{\mathfrak{g}}\mathfrak{g}^{\scriptscriptstyle(n)}=\mathcal{G}(\mathfrak{g},\partial\mathfrak{g},\dots,\partial^{n}\mathfrak{g})\text{,}

that is, the divergence and the trace of
\mathfrak{g}^{\scriptscriptstyle(n)}
are determined by
\mathfrak{g}
.
On the other hand, the remaining components of
\mathfrak{g}^{\scriptscriptstyle(n)}
are free—they are not formally determined by the Einstein-vacuum equations.
Moreover, assuming sufficient regularity for
\mathsf{g}(\rho)
, the expansion (
1.6
) can be continued beyond
\mathfrak{g}^{\scriptscriptstyle(n)}
, with all subsequent coefficients formally determined by the pair
(\mathfrak{g}^{\scriptscriptstyle(0)},\mathfrak{g}^{\scriptscriptstyle(n)})
alone.
5
5

5

When
n
is even, the expansion remains polyhomogeneous beyond
\mathfrak{g}^{\scriptscriptstyle(n)}
.

Thus, we henceforth refer to
(\mathscr{I},\mathfrak{g}^{\scriptscriptstyle(0)},\mathfrak{g}^{\scriptscriptstyle(n)})
as
holographic data
, or a
boundary triple
, if
\mathfrak{g}^{\scriptscriptstyle(0)}
is a Lorentzian metric on
\mathscr{I}
and
\mathfrak{g}^{\scriptscriptstyle(n)}
is a symmetric
2
-tensor on
\mathscr{I}
satisfying (
1.7
).
6
6

6

Our terminology arises from the common description of the AdS/CFT correspondence in physics as “holographic”. This is due to the difference in dimension between the aAdS spacetime and its conformal boundary.

Remark 1.2
.

The interpretation of
\mathfrak{g}^{\scriptscriptstyle(0)}
, as describing the geometry of the conformal boundary, is clear.
In addition, in the physics literature,
\mathfrak{g}^{\scriptscriptstyle(n)}
is closely connected to the stress-energy tensor for the boundary conformal field theory; see
[
20
,
51
]
for further discussions.

The expansions (
1.6
), which are widely used in the physics literature, can be formally derived by adapting the seminal works
[
22
]
of Fefferman and Graham to aAdS settings.
For
real-analytic
holographic data
(\mathscr{I},\mathfrak{g}^{\scriptscriptstyle(0)},\mathfrak{g}^{\scriptscriptstyle(n)})
, one can employ Fuchsian techniques to show
[
39
]
that the infinite expansion (
1.6
) converges near
\mathscr{I}
to a vacuum aAdS metric.
7
7

7

However, note this does not a priori prevent the possibility that there exist other vacuum metrics realising the boundary data whose Fefferman-Graham expansions do not converge.

For generic (non-analytic) settings, where the full expansion (
1.6
) needs not converge,
[
50
]
showed rigorously that a vacuum FG-aAdS segment must still satisfy a
partial FG expansion
.
More specifically,
\mathsf{g}(\rho)
retains the form (
1.6
), but only up to
n
-th order.
Nonetheless, the view of
(\mathscr{I},\mathfrak{g}^{\scriptscriptstyle(0)},\mathfrak{g}^{\scriptscriptstyle(n)})
as free boundary data (with the constraint (
1.7
)) for vacuum aAdS spacetimes persists.
A summary of the precise results of
[
50
]
can be found in Theorem
2.16
and Corollary
2.18
below.

1.1.1.
Gauge Covariance

The term conformal infinity arises from a special gauge covariance inherent to aAdS spacetimes.
Here, one can transform the boundary defining function
\rho
in a manner that preserves the FG gauge condition (
1.4
) but alters the corresponding FG expansion (
1.6
).
One can show that the boundary metric
\mathfrak{g}^{\scriptscriptstyle(0)}
then undergoes a conformal transformation,

(1.8)

\mathfrak{g}^{\scriptscriptstyle(0)}\mapsto\check{\mathfrak{g}}^{\scriptscriptstyle(0)}=e^{2\mathfrak{a}}\mathfrak{g}^{\scriptscriptstyle(0)}\text{.}

Thus, another way of phrasing this is that only the conformal class
[\mathfrak{g}]
of the induced boundary metric can be invariantly associated with a given aAdS spacetime.

The other coefficients in (
1.6
) are also transformed via changes of FG gauge (see
[
20
,
35
]
), though the formulas quickly become rather complicated.
In particular, there is a known, and in principle explicitly computable, function
\mathcal{H}
—depending on
\mathfrak{a}
,
\mathfrak{g}^{\scriptscriptstyle(0)}
, and
\mathfrak{g}^{\scriptscriptstyle(n)}
—such that
\mathfrak{g}^{\scriptscriptstyle(n)}
transforms as

(1.9)

\mathfrak{g}^{\scriptscriptstyle(n)}\mapsto\check{\mathfrak{g}}^{\scriptscriptstyle(n)}=\mathcal{H}(\partial^{\leq n}\mathfrak{a},\partial^{\leq n}\mathfrak{g}^{\scriptscriptstyle(0)},\mathfrak{g}^{\scriptscriptstyle(n)})\text{.}

As a result, we refer to pairs
(\mathfrak{g}^{\scriptscriptstyle(0)},\mathfrak{g}^{\scriptscriptstyle(n)})
and
(\check{\mathfrak{g}}^{\scriptscriptstyle(0)},\check{\mathfrak{g}}^{\scriptscriptstyle(n)})
as
gauge-equivalent
when they are related via the formulas (
1.8
) and (
1.9
).
The physical significance is that gauge-equivalent pairs should be viewed as “the same”, since they arise from the same aAdS spacetime.

Remark 1.3
.

The most general formulation of gauge equivalence can be expressed as two boundary data triples
(\mathscr{I},\mathfrak{g}^{\scriptscriptstyle(0)},\mathfrak{g}^{\scriptscriptstyle(n)})
and
(\check{\mathscr{I}},\check{\mathfrak{g}}^{\scriptscriptstyle(0)},\check{\mathfrak{g}}^{\scriptscriptstyle(n)})
satisfying (
1.8
), (
1.9
) after pulling back through some boundary diffeomorphism
\smash{\phi:\mathscr{I}\leftrightarrow\check{\mathscr{I}}}
.
However, for convenience, we will always restrict, without any loss of generality, to the case when
\phi
is the identity map.

1.2.
The Main Results

While the above discussion shows that any vacuum FG-aAdS segment induces some holographic data
(\mathscr{I},\mathfrak{g}^{\scriptscriptstyle(0)},\mathfrak{g}^{\scriptscriptstyle(n)})
, it is also natural to ask the converse—in what sense does the holographic data
(\mathscr{I},\mathfrak{g}^{\scriptscriptstyle(0)},\mathfrak{g}^{\scriptscriptstyle(n)})
determine an Einstein-vacuum metric that realises this data.
In view of the timelike nature of the boundary and the hyperbolicity of the Einstein-vacuum equations, this is generally an ill-posed problem, and hence one cannot expect existence and continuous dependence of the infilling geometry on the boundary quantities.

Instead, the appropriate mathematical framework is that of
unique continuation
for the Einstein-vacuum equations, leading us to the following more precise questions:

Problem 1.4
.

Given holographic data
(\mathscr{I},\mathfrak{g}^{\scriptscriptstyle(0)},\mathfrak{g}^{\scriptscriptstyle(n)})
—up to gauge equivalence for
(\mathfrak{g}^{\scriptscriptstyle(0)},\mathfrak{g}^{\scriptscriptstyle(n)})
—
and
a vacuum FG-aAdS segment
(\mathscr{M},g)
that realises this data:

(1)

Is
(\mathscr{M},g)
unique, that is, is this the only aAdS solution realising this holographic data?

(2)

Does
(\mathscr{M},g)
necessarily inherit the symmetries of
(\mathscr{I},\mathfrak{g}^{\scriptscriptstyle(0)},\mathfrak{g}^{\scriptscriptstyle(n)})
?

Note in particular that (1) in the above can be interpreted as asking
whether there is a one-to-one correspondence between vacuum aAdS spacetimes (gravity) and some appropriate space of holographic data on the conformal boundary (conformal field theory)
.

Our paper provides an
affirmative answer to both questions in Problem
1.4
, provided
the conformal boundary also satisfies a gauge-invariant geometric condition
—which we call the
generalised null convexity criterion
, or
GNCC
, first identified in
[
18
]
.
This GNCC will be defined and discussed in Section
1.3
below (Definition
1.12
), but let us first state informal versions of our main results.

The following theorem answers question (1) of Problem
1.4
:

Theorem 1.5

(Bulk-boundary correspondence, informal version)
.

Let
n>2
, and consider vacuum FG-aAdS segments
(\mathscr{M},g)
and
(\check{\mathscr{M}},\check{g})
, inducing holographic data
(\mathscr{I},\mathfrak{g}^{\scriptscriptstyle(0)},\mathfrak{g}^{\scriptscriptstyle(n)})
and
(\mathscr{I},\check{\mathfrak{g}}^{\scriptscriptstyle(0)},\check{\mathfrak{g}}^{\scriptscriptstyle(n)})
, respectively.
Also, let
\mathscr{D}\subset\mathscr{I}
such that
(\mathscr{D},\mathfrak{g}^{\scriptscriptstyle(0)})
satisfies the GNCC.
If
(\mathfrak{g}^{\scriptscriptstyle(0)},\mathfrak{g}^{\scriptscriptstyle(n)})
and
(\check{\mathfrak{g}}^{\scriptscriptstyle(0)},\check{\mathfrak{g}}^{\scriptscriptstyle(n)})
are gauge-equivalent on
\mathscr{D}
, then
(\mathscr{M},g)
and
(\check{\mathscr{M}},\check{g})
must be isometric near
\mathscr{D}
.

The precise version of Theorem
1.5
that we will prove is stated as Theorem
6.7
further below.
Furthermore, the special case in which
(\mathfrak{g}^{\scriptscriptstyle(0)},\mathfrak{g}^{\scriptscriptstyle(n)})=(\check{\mathfrak{g}}^{\scriptscriptstyle(0)},\check{\mathfrak{g}}^{\scriptscriptstyle(n)})
, which forms the heart of the unique continuation analysis, is treated separately in Theorem
5.1
.

Remark 1.6
.

Since
(\mathscr{M},g)
and
(\check{\mathscr{M}},\check{g})
have the same boundary manifold
\mathscr{I}
, by Definition
1.1
, both
\mathscr{M}
and
\check{\mathscr{M}}
are products of an interval with
\mathscr{I}
.
Therefore, we can, without loss of generality, assume
\mathscr{M}=\check{\mathscr{M}}
; we make this simplification in the statements of Theorems
5.1
and
6.7
.

1.2.1.
Extension of Symmetries

An important application of Theorem
1.5
toward proving extension of symmetry results on aAdS spacetimes—namely, point (2) from Problem
1.4
.

Theorem 1.7

(Extension of Killing fields, informal version)
.

Let
n>2
, consider a vacuum FG-aAdS segment
(\mathscr{M},g)
with holographic data
(\mathscr{I},\mathfrak{g}^{\scriptscriptstyle(0)},\mathfrak{g}^{\scriptscriptstyle(n)})
, and let
\mathscr{D}\subset\mathscr{I}
such that
(\mathscr{D},\mathfrak{g}^{\scriptscriptstyle(0)})
satisfies the GNCC.
If
\mathfrak{K}
is a vector field on
\mathscr{I}
that is
holographic Killing
on
\mathscr{D}
, that is,

(1.10)

(\mathscr{L}_{\mathfrak{K}}\mathfrak{g}^{\scriptscriptstyle(0)},\mathscr{L}_{\mathfrak{K}}\mathfrak{g}^{\scriptscriptstyle(n)})|_{\mathscr{D}}=0\text{,}

then
\mathfrak{K}
extends to a (
g
-)Killing field
K
near
\mathscr{D}
in
\mathscr{M}
.

See Theorem
6.11
for the precise statement of this result.
Moreover, the conclusion of Theorem
1.7
remains valid if
\mathfrak{K}
is merely
holographic conformal Killing
, that is, (
1.10
) holds instead for data
(\check{\mathfrak{g}}^{\scriptscriptstyle(0)},\check{\mathfrak{g}}^{\scriptscriptstyle(n)})
that is gauge-equivalent to
(\mathfrak{g}^{\scriptscriptstyle(0)},\mathfrak{g}^{\scriptscriptstyle(n)})
; see again Theorem
6.11
.

One immediate consequence of Theorem
1.7
and the classical Birkhoff theorem is the following rigidity result for the Schwarzschild-AdS family of spacetimes:

Corollary 1.8

(Rigidity of Schwarzschild-AdS)
.

Let
n>2
, let
(\mathscr{M},g)
denote a vacuum FG-aAdS segment with holographic data
(\mathscr{I}:=(T_{-},T_{+})\times\mathbb{S}^{n-1},\mathfrak{g}^{\scriptscriptstyle(0)},\mathfrak{g}^{\scriptscriptstyle(n)})
, and let
\mathscr{D}\subset\mathscr{I}
such that
(\mathscr{D},\mathfrak{g}^{\scriptscriptstyle(0)})
satisfies the GNCC.
If
\mathfrak{g}^{\scriptscriptstyle(0)}
and
\mathfrak{g}^{\scriptscriptstyle(n)}
are both spherically symmetric on
\mathscr{D}
, then
(\mathscr{M},g)
must be isometric to a domain of the Schwarzschild-AdS spacetime near
\mathscr{D}
.

Remark 1.9
.

The statement of Corollary
1.8
can be refined by noting that if
\mathfrak{g}^{\scriptscriptstyle(0)}=\mathfrak{g}_{\textrm{AdS}}
, then the GNCC holds for a time slab
\mathscr{D}:=\{t_{-}<t<t_{+}\}
whenever
t_{+}-t_{-}>\pi
(see Proposition
1.16
).

Next, Theorem
1.7
can in fact be viewed as a special case of a more general result:

Theorem 1.10

(Extension of symmetries, informal version)
.

Let
n>2
, consider a vacuum FG-aAdS segment
(\mathscr{M},g)
with holographic data
(\mathscr{I},\mathfrak{g}^{\scriptscriptstyle(0)},\mathfrak{g}^{\scriptscriptstyle(n)})
, and let
\mathscr{D}\subset\mathscr{I}
be such that
(\mathscr{D},\mathfrak{g}^{\scriptscriptstyle(0)})
satisfies the GNCC.
If
\phi
is a boundary diffeomorphism such that
(\phi_{\ast}\mathfrak{g}^{\scriptscriptstyle(0)},\phi_{\ast}\mathfrak{g}^{\scriptscriptstyle(n)})
and
(\mathfrak{g}^{\scriptscriptstyle(0)},\mathfrak{g}^{\scriptscriptstyle(n)})
are gauge-equivalent on
\mathscr{D}
,
8
8

8

Informally,
\phi
is a
holographic conformal symmetry
on
\mathscr{D}
.
then
\phi
extends to an isometry of
(\mathscr{M},g)
near
\mathscr{D}
.

See Theorem
6.9
for the precise statement and proof of this result.
In particular, Theorem
1.10
also applies to discrete symmetries that are not generated by Killing vector fields.
One immediate consequence of Theorem
1.10
—which cannot be inferred directly from Theorem
1.7
—is that time periodicity of the conformal boundary is inherited by the bulk spacetime:

Corollary 1.11

(Extension of time periodicity)
.

Let
n>2
, let
(\mathscr{M},g)
be a vacuum FG-aAdS segment with holographic data
(\mathscr{I},\mathfrak{g}^{\scriptscriptstyle(0)},\mathfrak{g}^{\scriptscriptstyle(n)})
, and let
\mathscr{D}\subset\mathscr{I}
such that
(\mathscr{D},\mathfrak{g}^{\scriptscriptstyle(0)})
satisfies the GNCC.
If
\mathfrak{g}^{\scriptscriptstyle(0)}
and
\mathfrak{g}^{\scriptscriptstyle(n)}
are both time-periodic on
\mathscr{D}
, then
(\mathscr{M},g)
must be time-periodic near
\mathscr{D}
.

1.2.2.
Previous and Related Work

The Riemannian analogue of Theorem
1.5
was proven by Biquard
[
14
]
using a Carleman estimate of Mazzeo
[
43
]
for asymptotically hyperbolic manifolds; see also
[
12
,
13
]
.
The work of Biquard was then generalised by Chrusciel and Delay
[
19
]
to an analogue of Theorem
1.5
, under the restriction that the spacetimes are
stationary
.
Also,
[
19
, Theorem 1.6]
is an analogue of our Theorem
1.10
, again assuming a priori that the spacetime is stationary.

We note that a fundamental ingredient in
[
12
,
13
,
14
,
19
]
is that the key equations are elliptic in nature.
In contrast, our main theorems, which are centred around hyperbolic equations, constitute the first correspondence and symmetry extension results in general dynamical settings.

We also recall, again in the Riemannian context, the well-known result of Graham and Lee
[
26
]
, which shows (for
n\geq 4
) existence of asymptotically hyperbolic Einstein metrics on the Poincaré ball
\mathbb{B}^{n}
with prescribed conformal infinity on the boundary, provided the boundary metric is sufficiently close (in the
C^{2,\alpha}
-norm) to the round metric on
\mathbb{S}^{n-1}
.
Note this corresponds to solving an elliptic Dirichlet problem, which has no analogue for hyperbolic equations.

In the Lorentzian context, we first mention the programme of Anderson
[
10
,
11
]
.
In
[
11
]
, a
conditional
global symmetry extension result for stationary Killing vectors was established under global a priori assumptions on
(\mathscr{M},g)
(including convergence to stationarity as
t\rightarrow\pm\infty
), assuming that a unique continuation property holds from
\mathscr{I}
for the linearized Einstein equations.

Moreover, extension results for Killing fields have seen several applications in general relativity.
For instance, (unconditional) Killing extension theorems have been established in the contexts of black hole rigidity
[
4
,
5
,
24
,
37
]
, cosmic censorship
[
47
]
, and non-existence of time-periodicity
[
6
]
.
The proofs of these results revolve around proving unique continuation for a system of tensorial wave and transport equations that is similar to the system studied in this paper; see Section
1.5.2
.

Returning to the aAdS setting,
[
18
,
30
,
31
,
45
]
established the first unique continuation results for (scalar and tensorial) wave equations, from the conformal boundaries of general dynamical aAdS spacetimes.
In particular, the Carleman estimates developed in
[
18
,
30
,
31
,
45
]
form a key ingredient for proving the main results of this paper; see Section
1.4
for further discussions in this direction.

Finally, the recent work of McGill
[
44
]
, which characterized locally AdS spacetimes in terms of its holographic data, can be seen as a precursor to our results and as a special case of Theorem
1.5
.
More specifically,
[
44
]
showed that (assuming the GNCC) a vacuum FG-aAdS segment
(\mathscr{M},g)
is locally AdS if and only if both
\mathfrak{g}^{\scriptscriptstyle(0)}
is conformally flat and
\mathfrak{g}^{\scriptscriptstyle(n)}=0
.
The key step in the proof of this result is a more straightforward analogue of the process in this paper; in particular,
[
44
]
applies directly the unique continuation results of
[
45
]
to the tensorial wave equations satisfied by the spacetime curvature on a single aAdS spacetime.

1.3.
The Generalised Null Convexity Criterion

We now turn our attention toward the key geometric assumption required for Theorems
1.5
,
1.7
, and
1.10
—the GNCC of
[
18
]
.
First, we give a rough statement of the GNCC, in the special case of vacuum aAdS spacetimes treated here:

Definition 1.12
.

Let
(\mathscr{M},g)
be a vacuum FG-aAdS segment, with conformal boundary
(\mathscr{I},\mathfrak{g})
, and consider an open subset
\mathscr{D}\subset\mathscr{I}
with compact closure.
We say
(\mathscr{D},\mathfrak{g})
satisfies the
generalised null convexity criterion
(or
GNCC
) iff there is a
C^{4}
-function
\eta
on a neighbourhood of
\bar{\mathscr{D}}
such that:

•

\eta>0
on
\mathscr{D}
, and
\eta=0
on the boundary
\partial\mathscr{D}
of
\mathscr{D}
.

•

The following bilinear form is uniformly positive-definite on
\mathscr{D}
along all
\mathfrak{g}
-null directions,

(1.11)

\eta^{-1}\mathfrak{D}^{2}_{\mathfrak{g}}\eta+\mathcal{P}[\mathfrak{g}]\text{,}

where
\mathfrak{D}^{2}_{\mathfrak{g}}
and
\mathcal{P}[\mathfrak{g}]
are the Hessian and Schouten tensor with respect to
\mathfrak{g}
, respectively.

Remark 1.13
.

One important feature of the GNCC is that it is conformally invariant.
In particular,
[
18
, Proposition 3.6]
showed that if
(\mathscr{D},\mathfrak{g})
satisfies the GNCC with
\eta
, then the conformally related
(\mathscr{D},e^{2\varphi}\mathfrak{g})
also satisfies the GNCC, with
\eta^{\prime}:=e^{\varphi}\eta
.

Remark 1.14
.

Observe that
\mathcal{P}[\mathfrak{g}]
can be replaced by
\frac{1}{n-2}\operatorname{Ric}[\mathfrak{g}]
in (
1.11
), since their difference is proportional to
\mathfrak{g}
and hence vanishes along all null directions.

Remark 1.15
.

One can also show
[
45
, Proposition 3.4]
that
(\mathscr{D},\mathfrak{g})
satisfies the GNCC if and only if there exists
\eta
as in Definition
1.12
and a smooth function
\zeta:\mathscr{I}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
such that the following bilinear form is uniformly positive-definite on
\mathscr{D}
along
all
directions:
9
9

9

This can be directly checked when
n=2
. For
n\geq 3
, this follows from the fact that two bilinear forms that do not vanish simultaneously (except at zero) can be simultaneously diagonalised
[
27
]
.

(1.12)

\eta^{-1}\mathfrak{D}^{2}_{\mathfrak{g}}\eta+\mathcal{P}[\mathfrak{g}]-\zeta\,\mathfrak{g}

See Definition
4.3
or
[
18
]
for a more precise description of the GNCC.
Roughly, one can interpret the GNCC as stating that the domain
\mathscr{D}
is “large enough” with respect to the geometry of
(\mathscr{I},\mathfrak{g})
.
Its main significance, demonstrated in
[
18
]
, is that it precisely captures the conditions on the conformal boundary that lead to pseudoconvexity of the near-boundary geometry.
More specifically, it ensures the level hypersurfaces of
\smash{f:=\frac{\rho}{\eta}}
are pseudoconvex in a small region of
\mathscr{M}
near
\mathscr{D}
.
This observation was a crucial ingredient in the Carleman estimates of
[
18
]
; see the discussions in Section
1.4
.

1.3.1.
Special Cases

To further flesh out Definition
1.12
, let us now consider the special case of the AdS conformal boundary
(\mathscr{I}_{\textrm{AdS}},\mathfrak{g}_{\textrm{AdS}})
of (
1.5
), which satisfies

(1.13)

\mathfrak{g}_{\textrm{AdS}}=-dt^{2}+\mathring{\gamma}_{n-1}\text{,}\qquad\mathcal{P}[\mathfrak{g}_{\textrm{AdS}}]=\frac{1}{2}(dt^{2}+\mathring{\gamma}_{n-1})\text{.}

In addition, we take
\mathscr{D}:=\mathscr{D}_{0}
to be the time slab

(1.14)

\mathscr{D}_{0}:=(t_{-},t_{+})\times\mathbb{S}^{n-1}\text{,}\qquad\partial\mathscr{D}_{0}=\{t_{-},t_{+}\}\times\mathbb{S}^{n-1}\text{,}\qquad T_{-}<t_{-}<t_{+}<T_{+}\text{.}

Proposition 1.16

(
[
18
]
, Corollary 3.14)
.

(\mathscr{D}_{0},\mathfrak{g}_{\textrm{AdS}})
satisfies the GNCC if and only if
t_{+}-t_{-}>\pi
.

The key observation here is that if we assume
\eta
to depend only on
t
, then (
1.13
) yields

(1.15)

(\eta^{-1}\mathfrak{D}^{2}_{\mathfrak{g}}\eta+\mathcal{P}[\mathfrak{g}])(\mathfrak{Z},\mathfrak{Z})=\eta^{-1}(\mathfrak{Z}t)^{2}\,(\ddot{\eta}+\eta)\text{.}

Then, one can directly check that Definition
1.12
is satisfied by the function

(1.16)

\eta:=\sin\left(\pi\cdot\frac{t-t_{-}}{t_{+}-t_{-}}\right)

whenever
t_{+}-t_{-}>\pi
.
10
10

10

In particular, the condition
t_{+}-t_{-}>\pi
is required for the right-hand side of (
1.15
) to be positive.

(Conversely, if
t_{+}-t_{-}\leq\pi
, then a contradiction argument using Sturm comparison yields that the GNCC cannot hold for
(\mathscr{D}_{0},\mathfrak{g}_{\textrm{AdS}})
; see
[
18
, Lemma 3.7]
.)

Remark 1.17
.

Note in particular that Proposition
1.16
applies to every Kerr-AdS spacetime, since these all induce the AdS conformal boundary.

Remark 1.18
.

The key consequence of Proposition
1.16
—that unique continuation for wave equations holds from
\mathscr{D}_{0}
when
t_{+}-t_{-}>\pi
—was first proved as a special case of the results of
[
30
]
.

Next, we move to more general boundary domains that are foliated by a time function
t
,

(1.17)

\mathscr{I}_{\ast}:=(T_{-},T_{+})\times\mathcal{S}\text{,}\qquad\mathscr{D}_{\ast}:=(t_{-},t_{+})\times\mathcal{S}\text{,}

with
\mathcal{S}
being a compact manifold of dimension
n-1
.
Previous unique continuation results for linear wave equations were developed in this setting (
1.17
), and these can also be viewed as special cases of the GNCC.
First,
[
30
]
developed an analogue of the GNCC for static
\mathfrak{g}
.
This was extended to non-static
\mathfrak{g}
in
[
31
]
, and then to a wider class of metrics
\mathfrak{g}
and time foliations in
[
45
]
.

Let us focus on the key criterion of
[
45
]
, as well as its relation to the GNCC:

Proposition 1.19

(
[
18
]
, Proposition 3.13)
.

Assume the setting of (
1.17
), and suppose there exist constants
0\leq B<C
such that the following holds for any
\mathfrak{g}
-null vector field
\mathfrak{Z}
:
11
11

11

Observe that the second condition of (
1.18
) can be viewed as a bound on the non-stationarity of
\mathfrak{g}
, since
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{g}}^{2}t
is proportional to the Lie derivative of
\mathfrak{g}
along the gradient of
t
.

(1.18)

\mathcal{P}[\mathfrak{g}](\mathfrak{Z},\mathfrak{Z})\geq C^{2}\cdot(\mathfrak{Z}t)^{2}\text{,}\qquad|\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{g}}^{2}t(\mathfrak{Z},\mathfrak{Z})|\leq 2B\cdot(\mathfrak{Z}t)^{2}\text{.}

Then,
(\mathscr{D}_{\ast},\mathfrak{g})
satisfies the GNCC
as long as
t_{+}-t_{-}
is large enough (depending on
B
and
C
).

The proof of Proposition
1.19
is similar to that of Proposition
1.16
, except one now chooses
\eta
(still depending only on
t
) to roughly solve a damped harmonic oscillator:

(1.19)

\ddot{\eta}-2b|\dot{\eta}|+c^{2}\eta=0\text{,}\qquad B\leq b<c<C\text{.}

Remark 1.20
.

The connection between damped harmonic oscillators (
1.19
) and unique continuation from
\mathscr{D}_{\ast}
was first illuminated in
[
31
]
; see the discussions therein.

Remark 1.21
.

Proposition
1.19
can be used to generalize the conclusions of Proposition
1.16
:

•

For instance, if
t_{+}-t_{-}>\pi
, then Proposition
1.19
implies that
(\mathscr{D}_{0},\mathfrak{g})
satisfies the GNCC whenever
\mathfrak{g}
is a sufficiently small perturbation of
\mathfrak{g}_{\textrm{AdS}}
.

•

If
\mathfrak{g}
is static with respect to
t
, and if the cross-sections
\mathcal{S}
have positive Ricci curvature, then
(\mathscr{D}_{\ast},\mathfrak{g})
satisfies the GNCC for sufficiently large
t_{+}-t_{-}
; see
[
31
, Proposition B.2]
.

The conditions (
1.18
) were first identified in
[
45
]
and were named the
null convexity criterion
(or
NCC
).
Proposition
1.19
shows that the GNCC indeed generalizes the NCC, both removing the need for a predetermined time function and allowing for a larger class of boundary domains
\mathscr{D}
.

One advantage of the NCC (
1.18
) is that it is easier to check than the rather abstract GNCC.
On the other hand, one shortcoming of (
1.18
) is that it fails to be conformally invariant, as a conformal transformation of
\mathfrak{g}
can cause (
1.18
) to no longer hold.
This makes the NCC undesirable for the main results of this paper and provides a key motivation for developing the GNCC.

1.3.2.
Geodesic Return

The necessity of
some
geometric condition in Theorem
1.5
was already conjectured in
[
30
,
31
]
, due to the special properties of AdS geometry near its conformal boundary.

On AdS spacetime, there exist null geodesics which propagate arbitrarily close to the conformal boundary
\mathscr{I}_{\textrm{AdS}}
, but only intersect
\mathscr{I}_{\textrm{AdS}}
at two points that are time
\pi
apart; see
[
30
, Section 1.2]
.
12
12

12

In terms of the standard embedding of AdS spacetime into the Einstein cylinder
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{S}^{n}_{+}
, these geodesics move forward in time and along great circles in the spatial component, both with constant speed.

One can then construct, via the geometric optics methods of Alinhac and Baouendi
[
9
]
, solutions to linear wave equations that are concentrated along such a family of geodesics.
13
13

13

One important caveat is that the methods of
[
9
]
only apply directly to wave operators
\Box_{g}+\sigma
with the conformal mass
4\sigma:=n^{2}-1
. The case of general
\sigma
will be treated in the upcoming work of Guisset
[
29
]
.

These solutions yield, for AdS spacetime, counterexamples to unique continuation for various linear wave equations when the data on the conformal boundary is imposed on a timespan of less than
\pi
(the return time of these null geodesics), between the start and end times of the geodesics.

Remark 1.22
.

We note that not every wave equation can have such counterexamples to unique continuation.
By Holmgren’s theorem
[
33
]
, if all the coefficients of the wave equation (including the principal part
g
) are real-analytic, then the above counterexamples cannot exist.

One can in fact view the GNCC as a generalization of the above intuitions for AdS spacetime to aAdS settings.
This observation was given in
[
18
, Theorem 4.1]
, which connected the GNCC to the trajectories of null geodesics near the conformal boundary.
In particular, given a spacetime null geodesic
\Lambda
that is sufficiently close to the conformal boundary and that travels over
\mathscr{D}
satisfying the GNCC,
[
18
, Theorem 4.1]
established that
\Lambda
must intersect the conformal boundary within
\mathscr{D}
(in either the future or past direction).
In other words, there cannot exist any near-boundary null geodesics that travel over
\mathscr{D}
but do not terminate at
\mathscr{D}
itself.
From this, one concludes that the Alinhac-Baouendi counterexamples of
[
9
]
in AdS cannot be constructed over
\mathscr{D}
.

Remark 1.23
.

In addition,
[
45
, Theorem 4.5]
established an analogue of
[
18
, Theorem 4.1]
for the NCC (
1.18
).
This was the first result in general aAdS settings that directly connected criteria for unique continuation to near-boundary null geodesic trajectories.

Consequently, the above discussions give us two justifications for the GNCC being the crucial condition for unique continuation of wave equations from the conformal boundary:

•

The GNCC rules out the known counterexamples to unique continuation for waves.

•

The GNCC implies pseudoconvexity, allowing for unique continuation results to be proved.

Remark 1.24
.

Though the GNCC is crucial to our proof of Theorem
1.5
, it is not known whether the methods of
[
9
]
extend to the Einstein-vacuum equations.
The construction of counterexamples to unique continuation in Theorem
1.5
when the GNCC is violated is a challenging problem.

Finally, we note that this connection between the GNCC and null geodesics can be used to show that
no subdomain
\mathscr{D}
of the planar AdS or toric AdS conformal boundaries can satisfy the GNCC
; see
[
18
, Corollary 3.10]
.
14
14

14

These are analogues of AdS, but with the spheres
\mathbb{S}^{n-1}
are replaced with
\mathbb{R}^{n-1}
or the flat torus
\mathbb{T}^{n-1}
.

In particular, on both planar and toric AdS spacetimes, there exist null geodesics that remain arbitrarily close to but never intersect the conformal boundary for all times.

1.4.
Proof Overview of Theorem
1.5

In this subsection, we provide an outline of the proof of Theorem
1.5
, our key result.
First, via an appropriate gauge transformation, we can assume

(1.20)

(\mathfrak{g}^{\scriptscriptstyle(0)},\mathfrak{g}^{\scriptscriptstyle(n)})=(\check{\mathfrak{g}}^{\scriptscriptstyle(0)},\check{\mathfrak{g}}^{\scriptscriptstyle(n)})

on
\mathscr{D}
, without any loss of generality; for details of this process, see Section
6.1
.

Furthermore, since we are only concerned with the near-boundary region, we can assume (see Remark
1.6
) that
\smash{\check{\mathscr{M}}=\mathscr{M}}
, so that the two aAdS metrics
g
and
\check{g}
take the forms.

(1.21)

g=\rho^{-2}[d\rho^{2}+\mathsf{g}(\rho)]\text{,}\qquad\check{g}=\rho^{-2}[d\rho^{2}+\check{\mathsf{g}}(\rho)]\text{.}

In light of (
1.21
), it suffices to show that

(1.22)

\mathsf{g}-\check{\mathsf{g}}=0\text{.}

Below, we discuss each of the three key components of the proof of Theorem
1.5
.

1.4.1.
The Vertical Tensor Calculus

The main objects of analysis in the proof of Theorem
1.5
are so-called
vertical tensor fields
.
These can be thought of as tensor fields on
\mathscr{M}
that are everywhere tangent to the level sets of
\rho
; an equivalent way to view vertical tensor fields is as
\rho
-parametrized families of tensor fields on
\mathscr{I}
.
See Section
2.1
for a more detailed development.

The simplest examples of vertical tensor fields are the
vertical metrics

\mathsf{g}
and
\check{\mathsf{g}}
.
As these define Lorentzian metrics on each level set of
\rho
, one can also define corresponding vertical connections
\mathsf{D}
and
\check{\mathsf{D}}
on
\mathscr{M}
, respectively.
Other vertical tensor fields are obtained by appropriate decompositions of spacetime quantities, such as the Weyl curvature
W
associated with
g
:
15
15

15

See Section
2.1
for precise coordinate conventions. Roughly, Latin letters
a,b,\dots
denote vertical components.

\mathsf{w}^{0}_{abcd}:=\rho^{2}\,W_{abcd}\text{,}\qquad\mathsf{w}^{1}_{abc}:=\rho^{2}\,W_{\rho abc}\text{,}\qquad\mathsf{w}^{2}_{ab}:=\rho^{2}\,W_{\rho a\rho b}\text{.}

One reason for formulating our main quantities as vertical tensor fields is that these, when viewed as
\rho
-parametrised tensor fields on
\mathscr{I}
, have a natural notion of limits at the conformal boundary—as
\rho\rightarrow 0
.
(For instance, the boundary limit of
\mathsf{g}
is the boundary metric
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{g}^{\scriptscriptstyle(0)}
.)
This allows one to easily connect quantities in the bulk spacetime with those on the conformal boundary.

Analogues of vertical tensor fields have been widely used in mathematical relativity,
16
16

16

These are usually formulated as
horizontal tensors
that are everywhere tangent to a foliation of spacelike submanifolds. Common examples include the connection and curvature components in a double null foliation.
but here we also extend these ideas beyond the standard uses.
In particular, since tensorial wave equations play a key role in the proof of Theorem
1.5
, we want to make sense of a spacetime wave operator
\bar{\Box}
applied to vertical tensor fields.
Furthermore, we aim to do this in a covariant manner, so that the usual operations of geometric analysis—such as Leibniz rules and integrations by parts—continue to hold.
As a result of this, we can present the analysis of vertical tensor fields in almost the exact same manner as corresponding analyses of scalar fields.

The difficulty in defining
\bar{\Box}
covariantly lies in making proper sense of
second, spacetime
derivatives of vertical tensor fields.
17
17

17

First spacetime derivatives can be straightforwardly defined by projecting spacetime covariant derivatives.

To get around this, we extend our calculus to
mixed tensor fields
—those that contain both spacetime and vertical components.
This allows us to make sense of the spacetime Hessian as adding spacetime components to a mixed field; see Section
2.3
for details.

Mixed tensor fields and extended wave operators
\bar{\Box}
originated from
[
49
]
and have been applied in aAdS contexts in
[
18
,
30
,
31
,
45
]
.
18
18

18

Similar notions were independently developed and used in
[
38
]
.

The full vertical (and mixed) tensor calculus, in the form shown in this paper, was first constructed in
[
45
,
50
]
and was also adopted in
[
18
]
.

Remark 1.25
.

An alternative approach is to decompose our quantities into scalar fields and derive an analogue of the wave-transport system used in
[
3
]
.
One disadvantage is that the unknowns are only locally defined, while we have to work with all of
\mathscr{D}
simultaneously.
In contrast, the vertical formalism allows us to present our arguments in a geometric and frame-independent manner.

1.4.2.
The Wave-Transport System

The strategy for obtaining (
1.22
) is to formulate
\mathsf{g}-\check{\mathsf{g}}
as an unknown in a closed system of (vertical) tensorial transport and wave equations, and to then apply the requisite unique continuation results to this system.

From the Gauss-Codazzi equations on level sets of
\rho
and from (
1.1
), one derives

(1.23)

\partial_{\rho}\mathsf{g}_{ab}=:\mathsf{m}_{ab}\text{,}\qquad\partial_{\rho}\mathsf{m}_{ab}=-2\mathsf{w}^{2}_{ab}+\rho^{-1}\mathsf{m}_{ab}+\frac{1}{2}\mathsf{g}^{cd}\mathsf{m}_{ad}\mathsf{m}_{bc}\text{,}\qquad\mathsf{D}_{b}\mathsf{m}_{ac}-\mathsf{D}_{a}\mathsf{m}_{bc}=2\mathsf{w}^{1}_{cab}\text{;}

analogous formulas also hold with respect to
\check{\mathsf{g}}
.
We wish to couple the transport equations (
1.23
) to wave equations satisfied by the Weyl curvature
W
(see Proposition
3.4
for precise formulas):

\Box_{g}W+2nW=W\cdot W\text{.}

Decomposing
W
into vertical components as before, we derive, for
\mathsf{U}\in\{\mathsf{w}^{\star},\mathsf{w}^{1},\mathsf{w}^{2}\}
,

(1.24)

\bar{\Box}\mathsf{U}+c_{\mathsf{U}}\mathsf{U}=\mathsf{NL}(\mathsf{g},\mathsf{m},\mathsf{D}\mathsf{m},\mathsf{w}^{\star},\mathsf{w}^{1},\mathsf{w}^{2},\mathsf{D}\mathsf{w}^{\star},\mathsf{D}\mathsf{w}^{1},\mathsf{D}\mathsf{w}^{2})\text{,}

where
c_{\mathsf{U}}\in\mathbb{Z}
depends on the component
\mathsf{U}
considered.
Moreover,
\mathsf{NL}(\cdot)
represents terms involving (contractions of) the listed quantities that decay sufficiently quickly toward the conformal boundary, while
\mathsf{w}^{\star}
in (
1.24
) is a renormalization of
\mathsf{w}^{0}
; see (
3.2
) and (
3.6
) for precise formulas.

Remark 1.26
.

That different masses
c_{\mathsf{U}}
appear in (
1.24
), at least when
n>3
, is because
\mathsf{w}^{\star}
,
\mathsf{w}^{1}
, and
\mathsf{w}^{2}
have different asymptotics (in powers of
\rho
) at the conformal boundary.
19
19

19

The case
n=3
is an exception, as
c_{\mathsf{w}^{1}}=c_{\mathsf{w}^{2}}=2
, and
\mathsf{w}^{\star}
is fully determined by
\mathsf{w}^{1}
and
\mathsf{w}^{2}
.

One consequence of this is that we must treat the components
\mathsf{w}^{\star}
,
\mathsf{w}^{1}
,
\mathsf{w}^{2}
separately in our analysis.

Subtracting (
1.23
)-(
1.24
) from their counterparts for
\check{\mathsf{g}}
yields a closed wave-transport system for the quantities
\mathsf{g}-\check{\mathsf{g}}
,
\mathsf{m}-\check{\mathsf{m}}
,
\mathsf{w}^{\star}-\check{\mathsf{w}}^{\star}
,
\mathsf{w}^{1}-\check{\mathsf{w}}^{1}
, and
\mathsf{w}^{2}-\check{\mathsf{w}}^{2}
.
However this system
fails to close for the purpose of applying our Carleman estimates
.
In particular, the wave equation will only allow us to control up to one derivative of
\mathsf{w}^{\star}-\check{\mathsf{w}}^{\star}
,
\mathsf{w}^{1}-\check{\mathsf{w}}^{1}
, and
\mathsf{w}^{2}-\check{\mathsf{w}}^{2}
, which, in turn, allows us to control only one derivative of
\mathsf{m}-\check{\mathsf{m}}
and
\mathsf{g}-\check{\mathsf{g}}
.
On the other hand, when we take a difference of the wave equations (
1.24
), we obtain a term involving the difference of
\bar{\Box}
and
\smash{\check{\bar{\Box}}}
, which contains second derivatives of
\mathsf{g}-\check{\mathsf{g}}
(since
\mathsf{U}
is a tensor) that we a priori cannot handle.
20
20

20

In principle, one may try to obtain estimates for two vertical derivatives of the metric from the structure equation involving
\mathsf{w}^{0}
(see the second equation in (
3.3
)) and the vertical Riemann curvature. However, this introduces other difficulties related to finding an appropriate gauge on the vertical slices.

The resolution, inspired by the symmetry extension result
[
37
]
of Ionescu and Klainerman, is to apply a careful renormalization of the system that eliminates the troublesome quantities.
(See also Section
1.5.2
below, where we compare our wave-transport system with that of
[
37
]
.)

The first crucial observation is that while
\mathsf{D}^{2}(\mathsf{g}-\check{\mathsf{g}})
is off limits, we can obtain improved control if one derivative is a curl.
In particular, the first and third equations in (
1.23
) yield, roughly,

\displaystyle\partial_{\rho}[\mathsf{D}_{db}(\mathsf{g}-\check{\mathsf{g}})_{ac}-\mathsf{D}_{da}(\mathsf{g}-\check{\mathsf{g}})_{bc}]

\displaystyle\sim\mathsf{D}_{d}[(\mathsf{D}_{b}\mathsf{m}_{ac}-\mathsf{D}_{a}\mathsf{m}_{bc})-(\check{\mathsf{D}}_{b}\check{\mathsf{m}}_{ac}-\check{\mathsf{D}}_{a}\check{\mathsf{m}}_{bc})]

\displaystyle\sim\mathsf{D}_{d}(\mathsf{w}^{1}-\check{\mathsf{w}}^{1})_{cab}\text{.}

The above still does not quite suffice, and we need one more renormalization—this is due to terms involving
\mathsf{D}(\check{\mathsf{D}}-\mathsf{D})
, which again contain the undesirable
\mathsf{D}^{2}(\mathsf{g}-\check{\mathsf{g}})
.
All this leads us to define the auxiliary quantities (see (
3.11
) and (
3.12
) for precise formulas)

(1.25)

\displaystyle\mathsf{B}_{cab}

\displaystyle:=\mathsf{D}_{c}(\mathsf{g}-\check{\mathsf{g}})_{ab}-\mathsf{D}_{a}(\mathsf{g}-\check{\mathsf{g}})_{cb}-\mathsf{D}_{b}\mathsf{Q}_{ca}\text{,}

\displaystyle\partial_{\rho}\mathsf{Q}_{ca}

\displaystyle:=\mathsf{g}^{de}\mathsf{m}_{ce}(\mathsf{g}-\check{\mathsf{g}})_{ad}-\mathsf{g}^{de}\mathsf{m}_{ae}(\mathsf{g}-\check{\mathsf{g}})_{cd}\text{,}

with
\mathsf{Q}\rightarrow 0
as
\rho\rightarrow 0
.
We then show that
\mathsf{D}\mathsf{B}
can indeed be adequately controlled by
\mathsf{D}(\mathsf{w}^{1}-\check{\mathsf{w}}^{1})
.

The second crucial observation comes from a detailed examination of the difference
\smash{\bar{\Box}-\check{\bar{\Box}}}
.
To appreciate this, we consider the wave equation for just
\mathsf{w}^{2}-\check{\mathsf{w}}^{2}
for concreteness:

(1.26)

\bar{\Box}(\mathsf{w}^{2}-\check{\mathsf{w}}^{2})=\bar{\Box}\mathsf{w}^{2}-\check{\bar{\Box}}\check{\mathsf{w}}^{2}-(\bar{\Box}-\check{\bar{\Box}})\check{\mathsf{w}}^{2}\text{.}

The dangerous terms arise from the following (rather long) computation,

(1.27)

(\bar{\Box}-\check{\bar{\Box}})\check{\mathsf{w}}^{2}_{ab}=-\frac{1}{2}\rho^{2}\mathsf{g}^{cd}\mathsf{g}^{ef}\check{\mathsf{w}}^{2}_{eb}\mathsf{D}_{c}\mathsf{B}_{afd}-\frac{1}{2}\mathsf{g}^{ef}\check{\mathsf{w}}^{2}_{eb}\bar{\Box}(\mathsf{g}-\check{\mathsf{g}}+\mathsf{Q})_{af}+\{a\leftrightarrow b\}+\mathsf{Err}\text{,}

where
\{a\leftrightarrow b\}
denotes the preceding terms repeated but with
a
and
b
interchanged, and where
\mathsf{Err}
consists of (many) terms containing only difference quantities that we can control.

The key point is that the only instances of
\mathsf{D}^{2}(\mathsf{g}-\check{\mathsf{g}})
appear either as
\mathsf{D}\mathsf{B}
, which we can control, or as
\bar{\Box}
applied to difference quantities.
This leads us to the renormalized curvature difference

(1.28)

\mathsf{W}^{2}_{ab}:=\mathsf{w}^{2}_{ab}-\check{\mathsf{w}}^{2}_{ab}+\frac{1}{2}\mathsf{g}^{de}\check{\mathsf{w}}^{2}_{ad}(\mathsf{g}-\check{\mathsf{g}}+\mathsf{Q})_{be}+\frac{1}{2}\mathsf{g}^{de}\check{\mathsf{w}}^{2}_{db}(\mathsf{g}-\check{\mathsf{g}}+\mathsf{Q})_{ae}\text{,}

which in essence shifts the
\bar{\Box}
-terms from the right-hand side of (
1.27
) into the left; one can also define the remaining
\mathsf{W}^{1}
and
\mathsf{W}^{\star}
similarly.
In light of (
1.26
) and (
1.27
), we obtain that
\mathsf{W}^{\star}
,
\mathsf{W}^{1}
,
\mathsf{W}^{2}
satisfy wave equations that do not contain
\mathsf{D}^{2}(\mathsf{g}-\check{\mathsf{g}})
as sources.

Finally, the renormalized wave-transport system is obtained by treating the quantities

(1.29)

\mathsf{g}-\check{\mathsf{g}}\text{,}\qquad\mathsf{Q}\text{,}\qquad\mathsf{m}-\check{\mathsf{m}}\text{,}\qquad\mathsf{B}\text{,}\qquad\mathsf{W}^{\star}\text{,}\qquad\mathsf{W}^{1}\text{,}\qquad\mathsf{W}^{2}

as unknowns.
In particular, from the above discussions, and from various asymptotic properties of geometric quantities, we arrive at the (schematic) transport equations

(1.30)

\displaystyle\partial_{\rho}(\mathsf{g}-\check{\mathsf{g}})

\displaystyle=\mathsf{m}-\check{\mathsf{m}}\text{,}

\displaystyle\partial_{\rho}\mathsf{Q}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}(\rho)\,(\mathsf{g}-\check{\mathsf{g}},\mathsf{Q})\text{,}

\displaystyle\partial_{\rho}\mathsf{B}

\displaystyle=2(\mathsf{w}^{1}-\check{\mathsf{w}}^{1})+\mathcal{O}(\rho)\,(\mathsf{g}-\check{\mathsf{g}},\mathsf{Q},\mathsf{B})+\mathcal{O}(1)\,(\mathsf{m}-\check{\mathsf{m}})\text{,}

\displaystyle\partial_{\rho}[\rho^{-1}(\mathsf{m}-\check{\mathsf{m}})]

\displaystyle=-2\rho^{-1}\mathsf{W}^{2}+\mathcal{O}(1)\,(\mathsf{g}-\check{\mathsf{g}},\mathsf{m}-\check{\mathsf{m}},\mathsf{Q})\text{,}

coupled to the following (schematic) wave equations for any
\mathsf{W}\in\{\mathsf{W}^{\star},\mathsf{W}^{1},\mathsf{W}^{2}\}
:

(1.31)

\displaystyle\bar{\Box}\mathsf{W}+c_{\mathsf{W}}\mathsf{W}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathsf{V}\in\{{\mathsf{W}^{\star}},\mathsf{W}^{1},\mathsf{W}^{2}\}}\left[\mathcal{O}(\rho^{2})\mathsf{V}+\mathcal{O}(\rho^{3})\mathsf{D}\mathsf{V}\right]+\mathcal{O}(\rho)\,(\mathsf{m}-\check{\mathsf{m}})

\displaystyle\qquad+\mathcal{O}(\rho^{2})\,(\mathsf{g}-\check{\mathsf{g}},\mathsf{Q},\mathsf{D}(\mathsf{g}-\check{\mathsf{g}}),\mathsf{D}\mathsf{Q},\mathsf{D}(\mathsf{m}-\check{\mathsf{m}}),\mathsf{D}\mathsf{B})\text{,}

The
\mathcal{O}(\cdot)
’s in (
1.30
)–(
1.31
) indicate the asymptotics of various coefficients as
\rho\rightarrow 0
.

For more precise formulas, see Propositions
3.13
and
3.14
.
In particular, the wave-transport system (
1.30
)–(
1.31
) indeed closes from the point of view of derivatives.
21
21

21

The system (
1.30
)–(
1.31
) could also be used to derive general unique continuation results for the Einstein equations near general timelike hypersurfaces, providing an alternate approach to that of
[
1
]
.

1.4.3.
The Carleman Estimate

The technical workhorse in the proof of Theorem
1.5
, connecting the system (
1.30
)–(
1.31
) with unique continuation, is a Carleman estimate for wave equations that are satisfied by vertical tensor fields near aAdS conformal boundaries.

The role of Carleman estimates in unique continuation theory has an extensive history, tracing back to the seminal
[
15
,
17
]
for elliptic problems.
Classical results for wave equations—see
[
34
,
41
]
—highlight
pseudoconvexity
as the crucial condition needed for Carleman estimates, and hence unique continuation results, to hold across a given hypersurface.
The novelty in aAdS settings is that the conformal boundary is
zero-pseudoconvex
, so the classical results no longer apply.
22
22

22

In particular, the null geodesics with respect to
\rho^{2}g
asymptote toward being tangent to the boundary
\rho=0
. As a result, the conformal boundary just barely fails to be pseudoconvex.

These difficulties were overcome in a series of results
[
18
,
30
,
31
,
45
]
by the authors, Chatzikaleas, and McGill, leading to Carleman estimates and unique continuation results on FG-aAdS segments, under the assumption of the GNCC.
23
23

23

The machinery for deriving Carleman estimates in zero-pseudoconvex settings originated from works of the second author with Alexakis and Schlue
[
7
,
8
]
. See also
[
47
]
, which independently studied zero-pseudoconvex settings.

As mentioned before, the GNCC ensures the existence of a foliation of pseudoconvex hypersurfaces near the conformal boundary.
(See the above references for further discussions of the ideas leading to the Carleman estimates.)

We now give a rough statement of the wave Carleman estimate used in this article:

Theorem 1.27

(Carleman estimate for wave equations,
[
18
]
)
.

Let
(\mathscr{M},g)
be a vacuum FG-aAdS segment, and suppose its conformal infinity
(\mathscr{I},\mathfrak{g})
has a subdomain
\mathscr{D}\subset\mathscr{I}
such that
(\mathscr{D},\mathfrak{g})
satisfies the GNCC.
Also, fix
\sigma\in\mathbb{R}
, set
\smash{f:=\frac{\rho}{\eta}}
(with
\eta
as in Definition
1.12
), and define the region

\Omega_{f_{\star}}:=\{f<f_{\star}\}\subset\mathscr{M}\text{,}\qquad f_{\star}>0\text{.}

Then, the following holds for any vertical tensor field
\mathsf{\Phi}
on
\mathscr{M}
with
\mathsf{\Phi}
,
\nabla\mathsf{\Phi}
vanishing on
f=f_{\star}
,

(1.32)

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega_{f_{\star}}}e^{-\frac{\lambda f^{p}}{p}}f^{n-2-p-2\kappa}|(\bar{\Box}+\sigma)\mathsf{\Phi}|^{2}\,dg

\displaystyle\qquad+\lambda^{3}\limsup_{\rho_{\star}\searrow 0}\int_{\{\rho=\rho_{\star}\}}(|\partial_{\rho}(\rho^{-\kappa}\mathsf{\Phi})|^{2}+|\mathsf{D}(\rho^{-\kappa}\mathsf{\Phi})|^{2}+|\rho^{-\kappa-1}\mathsf{\Phi}|^{2})\,d\mathsf{g}

\displaystyle\quad\gtrsim\lambda\int_{\Omega_{f_{\star}}}e^{-\frac{\lambda f^{p}}{p}}f^{n-2-2\kappa}(f\rho^{3}|\mathsf{D}\mathsf{\Phi}|^{2}+f^{2p}|\mathsf{\Phi}|^{2})\,dg\text{,}

provided
\kappa
and
\lambda
are sufficiently large,
f_{\star}
is sufficiently small, and
0<p<\frac{1}{2}
.

Remark 1.28
.

The norm
|\cdot|
can be defined relative to a given Riemannian metric on the space of vertical tensors.
Moreover, the admissible values of
\kappa
,
\lambda
,
f_{\star}
depend on
\mathsf{g}
,
\mathscr{D}
,
\sigma
, and the rank of
\Phi
.

See Theorem
4.6
below for a precise statement of the Carleman estimate.
The region
\Omega_{f_{\star}}
, on which the Carleman estimate holds, is illustrated in Figure
1
.

Figure 1.
The slices of constant
f
and the region
\Omega_{f_{\star}}
.

The zero-pseudoconvexity of the conformal boundary leads to several complications in both the statement and the proof of Theorem
1.27
.
For instance, one consequence of this is that in contrast to classical results, which apply in small neighbourhoods of a single point, the estimate (
1.32
) holds only near sufficiently large domains
\mathscr{D}
in the conformal boundary that satisfy the GNCC.
This is a feature that is exclusive to zero-pseudoconvex settings.

A second complication, arising from the degeneration of the pseudoconvexity of the level sets of
f
toward the conformal boundary, is the presence of decaying weights in (
1.32
)—
f\rho^{3}
and
f^{2p}
in the right-hand side.
This makes absorption arguments in the proof of Theorem
1.27
far more delicate, and it restricts the class of wave equations for which one can prove unique continuation—namely, to equations with similarly decaying lower-order coefficients.

Remark 1.29
.

There do exist stronger unique continuation results, for which pseudoconvexity is not needed at all; see
[
36
,
40
,
48
,
52
]
.
However, these results require either additional symmetries or partial analyticity, neither of which is available in our setting.

Theorem
1.27
is supplemented by a Carleman estimate—with the same region
\Omega_{f_{\ast}}
and Carleman weight—for transport equations.
In contrast to Theorem
1.27
, the proof of the transport Carleman estimate is straightforward; see Proposition
4.9
below for the precise statement and for details.

1.4.4.
Unique Continuation

The last step is to apply the wave and transport Carleman estimates to our system (
1.30
)–(
1.31
) to derive unique continuation—in particular (
1.22
).
First, we claim
all the unknowns (
1.29
) vanish to arbitrarily high order at the conformal boundary
, so there are no boundary terms present in the Carleman estimates.
This follows from two key observations:

•

From (
1.20
), one can also derive that
\mathfrak{g}^{\scriptscriptstyle(k)}=\check{\mathfrak{g}}^{\scriptscriptstyle(k)}
for all
k<n
, and that
\mathfrak{g}^{\scriptscriptstyle(\star)}=\check{\mathfrak{g}}^{\scriptscriptstyle(\star)}
; see
[
50
]
for details.
This leads to high-order vanishing for all the unknowns (
1.29
).

•

Further orders of vanishing can then be derived from transport and Bianchi equations.
24
24

24

This can be viewed as coefficients of the two FG expansions matching beyond
\mathfrak{g}^{\scriptscriptstyle(n)}
and
\check{\mathfrak{g}}^{\scriptscriptstyle(n)}
.

See Section
5.1
for further details on these steps.

From here, the process is mostly standard.
We apply the wave Carleman estimate (
1.32
) to
\chi\mathsf{W}^{\star}
,
\chi\mathsf{W}^{1}
, and
\chi\mathsf{W}^{2}
—for an appropriate cutoff
\chi:=\chi(f)
—and recall (
1.31
) in order to obtain

(1.33)

\displaystyle\lambda\int_{\Omega_{i}} w_{\lambda}(f)\sum_{\mathsf{V}\in\{\mathsf{W}^{\star},\mathsf{W}^{1},\mathsf{W}^{2}\}}(\rho^{2p}|\mathsf{V}|^{2}+\rho^{4}|\mathsf{D}\mathsf{V}|^{2})\,dg

\displaystyle\quad\lesssim\int_{\Omega_{i}}w_{\lambda}(f)\sum_{\mathsf{U}\in\{\mathsf{g}-\check{\mathsf{g}},\mathsf{Q},\mathsf{m}-\check{\mathsf{m}},\mathsf{B}\}}(\rho^{2-p}|\mathsf{U}|^{2}+\rho^{4-p}|\mathsf{D}\mathsf{U}|^{2})\,dg

\displaystyle\quad\qquad+\int_{\Omega_{i}}w_{\lambda}(f)\sum_{\mathsf{V}\in\{\mathsf{W}^{\star},\mathsf{W}^{1},\mathsf{W}^{2}\}}(\rho^{4-p}|\mathsf{V}|^{2}+\rho^{6-p}|\mathsf{D}\mathsf{V}|^{2})\,dg+\int_{\Omega_{e}}w_{\lambda}(f)\,(\dots)\,dg\text{.}

where
\smash{w_{\lambda}(f):=e^{-\lambda p^{-1}f^{p}}f^{n-2-2\kappa}}
is the Carleman weight, and where
25
25

25

See (
5.15
)–(
5.16
) for the definition of
\chi
and its relation to
f_{i}
,
f_{e}
; in particular,
\chi
is non-constant precisely on
\Omega_{e}
.

\Omega_{i}:=\{0<f_{i}\}\text{,}\qquad\Omega_{e}:=\{f_{i}\leq f<f_{e}\}\text{,}\qquad 0<f_{i}<f_{e}<f_{\star}\text{.}

(The “
\dots
” in the
\Omega_{e}
-integral depends on the unknowns (
1.29
), various weights in
\rho
and
f
, and the cutoff
\chi
; however, its precise contents are irrelevant, as we only require that this integral is finite.)
Similar (but easier) applications of the transport Carleman estimate and (
1.30
) yield

(1.34)

\displaystyle\lambda\int_{\Omega_{i}} w_{\lambda}(f)\sum_{\mathsf{U}\in\{\mathsf{g}-\check{\mathsf{g}},\mathsf{Q},\mathsf{m}-\check{\mathsf{m}},\mathsf{B}\}}(|\mathsf{U}|^{2}+\rho^{3}|\mathsf{D}\mathsf{U}|^{2})\,dg

\displaystyle\quad\lesssim\int_{\Omega_{i}}w_{\lambda}(f)\sum_{\mathsf{U}\in\{\mathsf{g}-\check{\mathsf{g}},\mathsf{Q},\mathsf{m}-\check{\mathsf{m}},\mathsf{B}\}}(\rho^{2-p}|\mathsf{U}|^{2}+\rho^{5-p}|\mathsf{D}\mathsf{U}|^{2})\,dg

\displaystyle\quad\qquad+\int_{\Omega_{i}}w_{\lambda}(f)\sum_{\mathsf{V}\in\{\mathsf{W}^{\star},\mathsf{W}^{1},\mathsf{W}^{2}\}}(\rho^{2-p}|\mathsf{V}|^{2}+\rho^{5-p}|\mathsf{D}\mathsf{V}|^{2})\,dg\text{.}

The key point here is that the
\rho
-weights on the right-hand sides of (
1.33
) and (
1.34
) come from the
\mathcal{O}(\cdot)
-coefficients in (
1.30
)–(
1.31
).
The final crucial feature of our system is these
\rho
-weights are strong enough that, after summing (
1.33
) and (
1.34
),
the
\Omega_{i}
-integrals on the right-hand side can be absorbed into the left-hand side
(once
\lambda
is sufficiently large).
From the above, we conclude that

\lambda\int_{\Omega_{i}} w_{\lambda}(f)\sum_{\mathsf{V}\in\{\mathsf{W}^{\star},\mathsf{W}^{1},\mathsf{W}^{2}\}}\rho^{2p}|\mathsf{V}|^{2}\,dg+\lambda\int_{\Omega_{i}} w_{\lambda}(f)\sum_{\mathsf{U}\in\{\mathsf{g}-\check{\mathsf{g}},\mathsf{Q},\mathsf{m}-\check{\mathsf{m}},\mathsf{B}\}}|\mathsf{U}|^{2}\,dg\lesssim\int_{\Omega_{e}}w_{\lambda}(f)\,(\dots)\,dg\text{.}

Finally,
w_{\lambda}(f)
in the above can be removed in the standard fashion by noting that
w_{\lambda}(f)\leq w_{\lambda}(f_{i})
on
\Omega_{e}
and
w_{\lambda}(f)\geq w_{\lambda}(f_{i})
on
\Omega_{i}
.
The result (
1.22
) now follows by letting
\lambda\rightarrow\infty
.

1.5.
Comparison with Similar Results

It is instructive to compare the proof of Theorem
1.5
with those of some related results in the existing literature.

1.5.1.
Biquard’s Riemannian analogue

We recall
[
14
]
, which considered asymptotically hyperbolic Einstein manifolds
(M,g)
.
These (Riemannian) manifolds also have a conformal boundary
(\partial M,\mathfrak{g})
, as well as a Fefferman-Graham expansion from
\partial M
.
In this setting,
[
14
]
proved that
the coefficients
(\mathfrak{g}^{\scriptscriptstyle(0)},\mathfrak{g}^{\scriptscriptstyle(n)})
in the expansion uniquely determine the metric
g
on
M
—the analogue of Theorem
1.5
.

The main difference between Theorem
1.5
and
[
14
]
is that the key equations in the latter are elliptic.
Recall that all hypersurfaces are pseudoconvex in elliptic settings, hence the major difficulties of zero-pseudoconvexity and of constructing pseudoconvex hypersurfaces are entirely avoided.

Moreover,
[
14
]
can avoid working with the curvature directly, instead deriving a second order elliptic equation for the analogue of the second fundamental form
\mathsf{m}
, which sees arbitrary second derivatives of the metric
\mathsf{g}
on the right hand side.
26
26

26

This equation can be derived from analogues of (
1.23
) and the Bianchi equation for
\partial_{\rho}\mathsf{w}^{2}_{ab}
.

Since the equation is elliptic, the Carleman estimate for
\mathsf{m}
allows for controlling
two
derivatives of
\mathsf{m}
in terms of two derivatives of
\mathsf{g}
.
Also, as the (commuted) transport equation for
\mathsf{g}
estimates two derivatives of
\mathsf{g}
in terms of two derivatives of
\mathsf{m}
, the Carleman estimates already close at the level of the second fundamental form.

For our setting, the analogous equation for
\mathsf{m}
would be
hyperbolic
, and the Carleman estimates cannot be closed in the same way, since the hyperbolic version loses a derivative compared with the elliptic case.
Consequently, we must also introduce the curvature as an unknown, which greatly complicates both our system and the ensuing analysis.

1.5.2.
The Ionescu-Klainerman symmetry extension

Next, we look at
[
37
]
, which proved a symmetry extension result similar to Theorem
1.7
, but through finite hypersurface in a vacuum spacetime
(M,g)
.
In particular,
[
37
]
showed that a Killing vector field
K
on a domain
U\subset M
can be extended through a point
p\in\partial U
, provided
\partial U
is pseudoconvex near
p
.

The proof of this result begins by extending
K
along a geodesic vector field
X
through
\partial U
using the Jacobi equation.
One key step in showing that this extended
K
remains Killing is the derivation of a wave-transport system, on which a unique continuation result is applied:

(1.35)

\displaystyle\nabla_{X}\mathbf{B}

\displaystyle=c\cdot\mathbf{B}+c\cdot\mathbf{P}\text{,}

\displaystyle\nabla_{X}\mathbf{P}

\displaystyle=c\cdot\mathbf{A}+c\cdot\mathbf{B}+c\cdot\mathbf{P}\text{,}

\displaystyle\Box\mathbf{A}

\displaystyle=c\cdot\nabla\mathbf{P}+c\nabla\mathbf{B}+c\cdot\mathbf{P}+c\cdot\mathbf{B}+c\cdot{\bf A}\cdot{\bf A}\text{.}

Here, “
c\,\cdot
” denotes various contractions with tensorial coefficients, which we avoid specifying here.
The unknowns
\mathbf{B}
,
\mathbf{P}
,
\mathbf{A}
are spacetime tensor fields, roughly described as follows:

•

\mathbf{B}
consists of
\mathscr{L}_{K}g
plus a specially chosen antisymmetric renormalization term
\omega
, while
\mathbf{P}
consists of certain careful combinations of
\nabla\mathscr{L}_{K}g
and
\nabla\omega
:

\mathbf{B}_{\alpha\beta}:=\frac{1}{2}(\mathscr{L}_{K}g_{\alpha\beta}+\omega_{\alpha\beta})\text{,}\qquad\mathbf{P}_{\alpha\beta\mu}:=\nabla_{\alpha}\pi_{\beta\mu}-\nabla_{\beta}\pi_{\alpha\mu}-\nabla_{\mu}\omega_{\alpha\beta}\text{.}

•

\mathbf{A}
is a “modified Lie derivative” of the Weyl curvature
W
:

\mathbf{A}_{\alpha\beta\mu\nu}=\mathscr{L}_{K}W_{\alpha\beta\mu\nu}-(\mathbf{B}\odot W)_{\alpha\beta\mu\nu}\text{.}

That
K
is Killing follows from showing, via unique continuation, that
\mathbf{B}
,
\mathbf{P}
,
\mathbf{A}
all vanish.

There are two connections we can make between the system (
1.35
) and our results.
The first is that an analogous system can be applied to give a direct proof of Theorem
1.7
, without appealing to Theorem
1.5
.
Setting
X:=\rho\partial_{\rho}
and
K
to be extension of the Killing field from the conformal boundary, we obtain a system of the same form (
1.35
).
27
27

27

In aAdS settings,
\Box
is replaced by
\Box+2n
due to the cosmological constant.

However, we would also need to apply vertical decompositions to
\mathbf{B}
,
\mathbf{P}
,
\mathbf{A}
, since different components have different asymptotic behaviors at the conformal boundary.
Nonetheless, this decomposed system has the same qualities as (
1.30
)–(
1.31
), and we can similarly apply our Carleman estimates to this.

The second connection is that we can in fact draw a direct parallel between (
1.35
) and our wave-transport system (
1.30
)–(
1.31
).
One can construct a rough “dictionary” between the unknowns of
[
37
]
and our system by
replacing each
\mathscr{L}_{K}
applied to a quantity by the corresponding difference of that quantity for two metrics
.
More specifically, we identify the following:

•

\mathscr{L}_{K}g
in
[
37
]
corresponds to
\mathsf{g}-\check{\mathsf{g}}
in our paper.

•

The renormalized term
\omega
in
[
37
]
corresponds to our renormalization
\mathsf{Q}
.

•

The components of
\mathbf{P}
roughly map to both
\mathsf{m}-\check{\mathsf{m}}
and
\mathsf{B}
in our paper.

•

The modified Lie derivative
\mathbf{A}
corresponds to our renormalized curvature differences
\mathsf{W}^{\star}
,
\mathsf{W}^{1}
,
\mathsf{W}^{2}
.
Moreover,
\mathbf{B}\otimes W
connect directly to the renormalization terms in (
1.28
).

Remark 1.30
.

As a result of the above, the preceding discussions of our system (
1.30
)–(
1.31
) also help to explain the various renormalizations used in (
1.35
) and
[
37
]
.

1.6.
Further Questions

Finally, we conclude the introduction by discussing some further directions of investigation that are related to or raised by Theorem
1.5
.

1.6.1.
The Case
n=2
.

Recall Theorem
1.5
—and Theorems
1.7
and
1.10
by extension—all assume that the dimension
n
of the conformal boundary is strictly greater than
2
.
This raises the question of whether analogues of Theorems
1.5
,
1.7
, and
1.10
hold in the case
n=2
.

In fact, the problem simplifies considerably when
n=2
due to the rigidity of low-dimensional settings.
In particular, since the Weyl curvature vanishes identically in
3
dimensions, it follows already that any vacuum aAdS spacetime when
n=2
must be locally isometric to the (
3
-dimensional) AdS metric.
Furthermore, as all curvature terms disappear from the system (
1.23
), one can prove unique continuation using only transport equations (and avoiding wave equations).
This yields analogues of all our main theorems for
n=2
, but from
any
domain
\mathscr{D}
—
without requiring the GNCC
.
28
28

28

This is consistent with the fact that the Einstein-vacuum equations lose their hyperbolicity in (
2+1
)-dimensions.

1.6.2.
Optimal Boundary Conditions

An often studied setting in the physics literature is the case when the boundary region
\mathscr{D}\subset\mathscr{I}
in Theorem
1.5
is a causal diamond,
29
29

29

\mathcal{I}^{+}
and
\mathcal{I}^{-}
denotes the causal future and past, respectively, in
(\mathscr{I},\mathfrak{g})
.

(1.36)

\mathscr{D}:=\mathcal{I}^{+}(p)\cap\mathcal{I}^{-}(q)\text{,}\qquad p,q\in\mathscr{I}\text{.}

Unfortunately, one expects that causal diamonds (
1.36
), regardless of how large they are, should generically fail to satisfy the GNCC when
n>2
; see the argument in
[
18
, Section 3.3]
.
As a result, Theorem
1.5
fails to apply when
\mathscr{D}
is as in (
1.36
)—in other words, we cannot establish that vacuum aAdS spacetimes are uniquely determined by their boundary data on a causal diamond.

This leads to the question of whether the GNCC can be further refined, so that Theorem
1.5
can be somehow extended to apply to
\mathscr{D}
as in (
1.36
).
One observation here is that the failure of the GNCC is due only to the presence of corners in
\partial\mathscr{D}
where the boundaries of
\mathcal{I}^{+}(p)
and
\mathcal{I}^{-}(q)
intersect.
Near these corners, one can find near-boundary null (spacetime) geodesics “flying over” but avoiding
\mathscr{D}
.
This leads to the following question:
Could boundary data
(\mathfrak{g}^{\scriptscriptstyle(0)},\mathfrak{g}^{\scriptscriptstyle(n)})
on
\mathscr{D}
uniquely determine the vacuum aAdS spacetime near some proper subset
\mathscr{D}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathscr{D}
, in particular when
\mathscr{D}
is sufficiently large, and when
\mathscr{D}^{\prime}
is sufficiently far from any corners in
\partial\mathcal{I}^{+}(p)\cap\partial\mathcal{I}^{-}(q)
?

At the same time, one may ask whether this refined GNCC can also be formulated for more general domains
\mathscr{D}\subset\mathscr{I}
.
More specifically, one can formulate the following:

Problem 1.31
.

Consider the setting of Theorem
1.5
.
Show that if
\mathscr{D}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathscr{D}
satisfies some (yet to be formulated) “refined GNCC” relative to
\mathscr{D}
, then
(\mathscr{M},g)
is uniquely determined near
\mathscr{D}^{\prime}
by the boundary data
(\mathfrak{g}^{\scriptscriptstyle(0)},\mathfrak{g}^{\scriptscriptstyle(n)})
on
\mathscr{D}
, again up to gauge equivalence.

Keeping with the above intuitions, the optimal formulation of such a “refined GNCC” would be one that directly characterizes null geodesic trajectories near the conformal boundary.
Such a criterion would confirm the belief that unique continuation holds if and only if one cannot construct geometric optics counterexamples near the conformal boundary to unique continuation for waves, as in
[
9
]
.
However, a proof of such a statement may require incorporating, in a novel manner, ideas from microlocal analysis and propagation of singularities.

1.6.3.
Global Correspondences

Our main result, Theorem
1.5
, is “local” in nature, in the sense that the vacuum spacetime is only uniquely determined near the conformal boundary.
This is due to our rather general setup, which does not provide any information on the global spacetime geometry.
However, this leaves open the question of whether a more global unique continuation result can be established if more additional assumptions are imposed.

For example, one can consider aAdS spacetimes
(\mathscr{M},g)
that are global perturbations, in some sense, of a Kerr-AdS spacetime.
One can then ask whether the boundary data
(\mathfrak{g}^{\scriptscriptstyle(0)},\mathfrak{g}^{\scriptscriptstyle(n)})
determines the spacetime in the full domain of outer communications, or if additional conditions are needed to rule out bifurcating counterexamples.
In the positive scenario, another physical question of interest is whether one can construct a one-to-one correspondence between conformal boundary data
(\mathfrak{g}^{\scriptscriptstyle(0)},\mathfrak{g}^{\scriptscriptstyle(n)})
and some (appropriately conceived) data on the black hole horizon.

In
[
30
, Section 6]
, the authors applied the Carleman estimates of that paper to show that the linearized Einstein-vacuum equations on AdS spacetime (formulated as Bianchi equations for spin-
2
fields) is globally characterized by its boundary data on a sufficiently long time interval.
Upcoming work by McGill and the second author will extend this to the nonlinear setting—roughly, under additional global assumptions, AdS spacetime is globally uniquely determined, as a solution to (
1.1
), by its holographic boundary data.
An interesting next step would be to explore whether these analyses can be extended to black hole aAdS spacetimes.

1.7.
Organization of the Paper

In Section
2
, we provide a detailed development of vacuum FG-aAdS segments and our vertical tensor formalism.
Section
3
is dedicated to the wave-transport system that is at the heart of the proof of Theorem
1.5
, while Section
4
presents the key Carleman estimates for both wave and transport equations.
Finally, Section
5
proves the key unique continuation result for vacuum FG-aAdS segments, and Section
6
proves our main results: Theorems
1.5
,
1.7
, and
1.10
.
Finally, various proofs and derivations are presented separately in Appendix
7
.

Data Availability Statement

Data sharing is not applicable to this article as no datasets were generated or analysed during the current study.

Statement on Competing Interests

The authors have no financial or proprietary interests in any material discussed in this article.

2.
Preliminaries

This section is devoted to developing the background material that will be used throughout this article.
First, we give a precise description of the aAdS setting that we will study, and we state the assumptions we will impose on our spacetimes and their conformal boundaries.
We then turn our attention to Einstein-vacuum spacetimes, and we recall the Fefferman–Graham partial expansions derived in
[
50
]
.
In the remaining parts, we recall the mixed tensor fields introduced in
[
45
]
.
30
30

30

Similar notions were also used in
[
30
,
31
]
.

These are used to make sense of the wave operator
\bar{\Box}
applied to vertical tensor fields.
Finally, we derive various identities connecting vertical and spacetime geometric quantities.

2.1.
Asymptotically AdS Spacetimes

The first objective is to give precise descriptions of the aAdS spacetimes that we will study.
We begin with the background manifold itself:
31
31

31

Most of the material in this subsection can also be found in
[
45
, Sections 2.1–2.3]
.
We give an abridged discussion here for the purpose of keeping the present article self-contained.

Definition 2.1
.

An
aAdS region
is a manifold with boundary of the form

(2.1)

\mathscr{M}:=(0,\rho_{0}]\times\mathscr{I}\text{,}\qquad\rho_{0}>0\text{,}

where
\mathscr{I}
is a smooth
n
-dimensional manifold, and where
n\in\mathbb{N}
.
32
32

32

While we refer to
\mathscr{M}
as the aAdS region, this also implicitly includes the associated quantities
n
,
\mathscr{I}
,
\rho_{0}
.

Definition 2.2
.

Let
\mathscr{M}
be an aAdS region.
Then:

•

We let
\rho\in C^{\infty}(\mathscr{M})
denote the projection onto its
(0,\rho_{0}]
-component.

•

We let
\partial_{\rho}
denote the lift to
\mathscr{M}
of the canonical vector field
d_{\rho}
on
(0,\rho_{0}]
.

•

The
vertical bundle

\mathsf{V}^{k}_{l}\mathscr{M}
of rank
(k,l)
over
\mathscr{M}
is defined to be the manifold consisting of all tensors of rank
(k,l)
on each level set of
\rho
in
\mathscr{M}
:

(2.2)

\mathsf{V}^{k}_{l}\mathscr{M}=\bigcup_{\sigma\in(0,\rho_{0}]}T^{k}_{l}\{\rho=\sigma\}\text{.}

•

A (smooth) section of
\mathsf{V}^{k}_{l}\mathscr{M}
is called a
vertical tensor field
of rank
(k,l)
.

Remark 2.3
.

We adopt the following conventions and identifications on an aAdS region
\mathscr{M}
:

•

We use italicized font, serif font, and Fraktur font (for instance,
g
,
\mathsf{g}
, and
\mathfrak{g}
) to denote tensor fields on
\mathscr{M}
, vertical tensor fields, and tensor fields on
\mathscr{I}
, respectively.

•

Given
\sigma\in(0,\rho_{0}]
, we let
\mathsf{A}|_{\sigma}
be the tensor field on
\mathscr{I}
obtained from restricting
\mathsf{A}
to
\{\rho=\sigma\}
.

•

A vertical tensor field
\mathsf{A}
of rank
(k,l)
can be equivalently viewed as a one-parameter family,
\{\mathsf{A}|_{\sigma}\mid\sigma\in(0,\rho_{0}]\}
, of rank
(k,l)
tensor fields on
\mathscr{I}
.

•

Given a tensor field
\mathfrak{A}
on
\mathscr{I}
, we will also use
\mathfrak{A}
to denote the vertical tensor field on
\mathscr{M}
obtained by extending
\mathfrak{A}
as a
\rho
-independent family of tensor fields on
\mathscr{I}
.
33
33

33

In particular, a scalar function on
\mathscr{I}
also defines a
\rho
-independent function on
\mathscr{M}
.

•

Any vertical tensor field
\mathsf{A}
can be uniquely identified with a tensor field on
\mathscr{M}
(of the same rank) via the following rule: the contraction of any component of
\mathsf{A}
with
\partial_{\rho}
or
d\rho
(whichever is appropriate) is defined to vanish identically.

Finally, unless otherwise specfied, we always implicitly assume any given tensor field is smooth.

Definition 2.4
.

Let
\mathscr{M}
be an aAdS region.

•

We use the symbol
\mathscr{L}
to denote Lie derivatives of tensor fields, on both
\mathscr{M}
and
\mathscr{I}
.

•

We can also make sense of Lie derivatives of any vertical tensor field
\mathsf{A}
by treating it as a spacetime tensor field, as described in Remark
2.3
.

•

For convenience, we will often abbreviate
\mathscr{L}_{\partial_{\rho}}
as
\mathscr{L}_{\rho}
.

Remark 2.5
.

Observe that, in the context of Definition
2.4
, for any vertical tensor field
\mathsf{A}
, its Lie derivative
\mathscr{L}_{\rho}\mathsf{A}
can also be characterized as the unique vertical tensor field satisfying
34
34

34

(
2.3
) was the definition of
\mathscr{L}_{\rho}
used in
[
45
,
50
]
, and can be shown to be consistent with Definition
2.4
.

(2.3)

\mathscr{L}_{\rho}\mathsf{A}|_{\sigma}=\lim_{\sigma^{\prime}\rightarrow\sigma}(\sigma^{\prime}-\sigma)^{-1}(\mathsf{A}|_{\sigma^{\prime}}-\mathsf{A}|_{\sigma})\text{,}\qquad\sigma\in(0,\rho_{0}]\text{.}

In the following definitions, we establish conventions for coordinate systems and limits:

Definition 2.6
.

Let
\mathscr{M}
be an aAdS region, and let
(U,\varphi)
be a coordinate system on
\mathscr{I}
:

•

Let
\varphi_{\rho}:=(\rho,\varphi)
denote the corresponding lifted coordinates on
(0,\rho_{0}]\times U
.

•

We use lower-case Latin indices
a,b,c,\dots
to denote
\varphi
-coordinate components, and we use lower-case Greek indices
\alpha,\beta,\mu,\nu,\dots
to denote
\varphi_{\rho}
-coordinate components.
As usual, repeated indices indicate summations over the appropriate components.

•

(U,\varphi)
is called
compact
iff
\bar{U}
is a compact subset of
\mathscr{I}
and
\varphi
extends smoothly to
\bar{U}
.

•

Given a vertical tensor field
\mathsf{A}
of rank
(k,l)
, we define (with respect to
\varphi
-coordinates)

(2.4)
